Title: Fixed-Point Characterisations of Extremal Distributions under Partial Distributional Constraints

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.04315v1

Published Time: Thu, 06 Aug 2026 00:24:14 GMT

Number of Pages: 110

Markdown Content:
# Fixed-Point Characterisations of Extremal Distributions under Partial Distributional Constraints 

Kizito Salako 1 , Rabiu Tsoho Muhammad 1

> 1

The Centre for Software Reliability, Department of Computer Science, City St. George’s, University of London, Northampton Square, EC1V 0HB, The United Kingdom e-mail: k.o.salako@citystgeorges.ac.uk ; Rabiu-Tsoho.Muhammad@citystgeorges.ac.uk 

Abstract: We present a methodological framework for solving robust inference prob-lems with partially specified distributions over measurable subsets of a parameter space. Partial specifications define a set of admissible distributions. The goal is to determine extremal values (over these admissible distributions) for statistical quanti-ties, where these quantities—these objective functions—are ratios of expectations of analytic functions, continuous functions, piecewise continuous functions, and uniform limits of piecewise continuous functions. We show that extremal values are approached by sequences of admissible distributions, whose limiting extremal distributions are characterised by fixed-point conditions on their support locations. This characterises 

where extremal distributions place probability mass and yields a practical computa-tional framework for solving the corresponding optimisation problems. We establish convergence and asymptotic properties of the resulting extremal distributions and ex-tremal objective function values. This work extends robust inference methods (e.g. robust Bayesian inference) by combining extremal-distribution reduction, fixed-point characterisation, and approximation-based analysis within a unified framework. 

MSC2020 subject classifications: Primary 62F15, 62F35; secondary 90C32, 62F30. 

Keywords and phrases: robust Bayesian analysis, conservative Bayesian inference. 1arXiv:2608.04315v1 [math.ST] 5 Aug 2026 Contents

The following constrained optimisation problem arises often in robust inference settings. For 

d ∈ N, let K := [0 , 1] d and let K1, . . . , K n be a partition of K into Borel-measurable sets. Let D := 

{

P ∈ P (K) : P(Ki) = pi, i = 1 , . . . , n 

}

, where P(K) denotes the set of all Borel probability measures on K, pi > 0 ( i = 1 , . . . , n ), and ∑ni=1 pi = 1. Let f, g : K → [0 , ∞)be D-integrable; i.e. f, g ∈ L (P) for all P ∈ D . Further assume inf x∈Kj g(x) > 0 for some 

j ∈ { 1, . . . , n }. Then, the optimisation problem of interest is inf 

> P∈D

EP[f (X)] 

EP[g(X)] . (1) Problem (1) is solved when the infimum and a related extremal distribution of X are de-duced. It is natural to consider (1) within a Bayesian context, where the prior distribution of X is partially specified. Indeed, generalisations of (1) have been extensively studied in the robust Bayesian inference literature (see Berger (1990), Moreno and Cano (1991), Lavine (1991), Berger and Moreno (1994)), and certain nonlinear fractional program forms of (1) can be solved using Dinkelbach-type fixed–point iteration (see Dinkelbach (1967), Schaible (1976)). Beyond robust Bayes, (1) and its extensions also give generalised Boole–Fréchet bounds, including bounds under marginal-density constraints (where these bounds cannot be obtained directly via Hailperin’s classical linear-programming formulation; see Hailperin (1965)). Several special cases of (1) (and its generalisations in Section 2) have been solved and applied in various contexts; for example, see Laurence and Wang (2005), Bishop et al. (2011), Giacomini and Kitagawa (2021), Strigini and Povyakalo (2013), Rüger (1978), Zhao et al. (2019), Embrechts et al. (2013), Littlewood et al. (2020), Salako et al. (2021), Whitt (1976), Salako and Zhao (2023, 2024)). This paper makes three main methodological contributions to solving constrained infer-ence problems with partially specified distributions: i) it derives fixed-point characterisations of extremal priors for (1), showing that the optimisation can be reduced to discrete extremal priors, or limits of feasible priors, whose support points satisfy explicit Dinkelbach-type fixed-point conditions. This strengthens earlier extremal-prior reduction results by identi-fying where extremal priors place mass; ii) it develops these structural results into a prac-tical computational framework , based on discrete reformulations, fixed-point iteration, and approximation arguments, for solving (1) and several nontrivial generalisations (e.g. over-lapping measurable-set constraints and multiple marginal-density constraints); and iii) it proves convergence/asymptotic properties of the resulting extremal values and priors, ex-tending the framework beyond analytic objectives to continuous, piecewise continuous, and uniform-limit classes of functions. The paper is organised as follows. Section 2 develops the theoretical and computational framework for (1) and its generalisations. In particular, Subsection 2.1 establishes equiv-alent finite and discrete extremal-prior formulations, including extensions to overlapping measurable-set constraints and marginal-density constraints. Subsection 2.2 gives the main fixed-point characterisations and algorithms: analytic objectives are treated directly through Dinkelbach-type conditions, while continuous, piecewise continuous, and uniform-limit ob-jective classes are handled through approximation and convergence arguments. Section 3 then illustrates the framework with applications to one- and two-dimensional extremal-prior problems, Markov chain likelihoods with mixed prior constraints, Bernoulli reliability models, generalised Boole–Fréchet bounds, interval-identified econometric inference, robust 4value-at-risk aggregation, model-free basket-option bounds, and inference using Markov chains with random transition probabilities. Section 4 discusses the interpretation of the resulting extremal priors, their discrete or degenerate nature, their role in conservative Bayesian inference, their convergence to ordinary Bayesian inference under refined partial specifications, and the attractor–repeller structure that governs extremal support locations. 

# 2. Methodology 

This section establishes a general framework for solving (1) and its extensions. The key steps are: reduce the original optimisation to an extremal discrete problem, characterise the support of the resulting extremal priors via fixed-point conditions, then extend this to broader prior constraints and objective-function classes. The following are the structural foundation and computational tools for the framework. Four theorems give solutions of (1) under four nested classes of f, g functions: i) Theo-rem 2.9 for analytic functions; ii) Theorem 2.11 for continuous functions; iii) Theorem 2.13 for bounded piecewise continuous functions with a measurable set of discontinuities; iv) The-orem 2.15 for uniform limits of bounded piecewise continuous functions. Theorems 3.1–3.4, 3.6, 3.7 exemplify the general theorems’ solutions. The following definition is useful in what follows. 

Definition 2.1 (Dinkelbach difference ). hφ,f,g (x) := f (x) − φg (x) for φ ⩾ 0, x ∈ K

Remarks: If q = 0, the support bound reduces to n from n + q. Since pi > 0 for every 

i = 1 , . . . , n , any P ∈ D (n) 

> p, m

must place exactly one support point in each partition element 

Ki. The q moment constraints add at most q additional support points in total. 6Proposition 2.2 is a finite-support reduction for fractional objectives under fixed parti-tion probabilities and finitely many moment equalities. Its proof uses a finite-dimensional barycentric representation followed by a rank-pruning argument. When no moment con-straints are present, it recovers the finite-partition reduction of Moreno et al. (Moreno and Cano, 1991, Theorem 1). More generally, it is a structured equality-constraint instance of the generalized moment framework of Betrò et al. (Betrò et al., 1994, Theorem 3), itself based on Winkler’s extreme-point theorem (Winkler, 1988, Theorem 2.1). The present for-mulation differs by: i) exploiting the affine dependence among the n partition indicators, while incorporating the q moment constraints, to obtain the improved n + q support bound; 

ii) proving the stronger pointwise objective-value equivalence/domination statement (3). The two parts of (3) play different roles. The equality involving Q0 is a finite-moment representation statement: after fixing P, one may add one further moment constraint en-coding the objective value attained by P. This gives the ( n + q + 1)-atomic representative 

Q0 with the same objective value, by Richter’s theorem (Richter, 1957, Satz 4). However, this equality-preserving argument alone does not yield the sharper ( n + q)-atomic domina-tion statement involving Q: preserving the additional objective-value constraint costs one atom, whereas omitting it leaves the direction of the objective change uncontrolled. The proposition’s proof resolves this obstruction by a rank-pruning step: it preserves only the original partition and moment constraints while pruning one atom in a direction that does not increase the objective value, thereby producing Q.Proposition 2.3 shows that the partition in (1) is not as restrictive as it first appears. It is valid when finite constraints “overlap”.

Remark: Lavine et al. (1991) prove convergence of product-cell discretisations for fixed-marginal upper-bounds on expectations of uniformly continuous functions. This overlaps with a special case of Proposition 2.6. However, Proposition 2.6 generalises this overlap to approximating fractional objectives with approximation error that depends on the oscil-lations of the numerator and denominator of the objective function. Lavine et al. require the functions involved to be uniformly continuous, but they allow more general prescribed marginal measures than the density marginals considered here. Corollaries 2.7 and 2.8 restrict Proposition 2.6, respectively, to continuous functions and piecewise-defined functions whose oscillations vanish asymptotically over recursively refined partitions of K.

## 2.2. Solutions to Problem (1) and Its Generalisations 

The following Theorems 2.9, 2.11, 2.13, 2.15 present solutions and solution methods for (1) and its generalisations (Propositions 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 2.6).

Remarks: Algorithm 2.10 is a Dinkelbach-type iteration for Theorem 2.9 solutions: ̂ ϕt con-verges to ϕ∗, and any accumulation point of the sequence {(̂ ϕt,̂ x(t)1 , . . . , ̂ x(t) 

> n

)}t∈Z⩾0 satisfies the fixed-point conditions (E1) and (E2). For a proof of the convergence, see Appendix H of the Supplementary Material (Salako and Muhammad (2026)). Dinkelbach-type iteration is a classical method for solving nonlinear fractional programming problems. Likewise, fi-nite linear-fractional programmes admit the Charnes–Cooper transformation (see Charnes and Cooper (1962)). In the robust Bayesian setting, Cozman (1999) explicitly identified Lavine-type posterior-bound bracketing with Dinkelbach-type fractional programming, and the White–Snow reformulation with the Charnes–Cooper transformation. Thus, when the support points are fixed and the admissible probabilities are described by finite linear con-straints, this programme falls within the Cozman/fractional-programming setting. 13 K = [0 , 1] 2

More generally, the support points of extremal priors are unknown and must themselves be determined. Following the finite-support reduction in Proposition 2.2, Theorem 2.9 iden-tifies the extremal support configurations as those satisfying the fixed-point conditions (E1)– (E2). The subsequent extensions give approximation and convergence guarantees for broader function classes, overlapping-set constraints, and marginal-density settings.

Remarks: Together, Theorem 2.9 and Algorithm 2.10 are the basis of a general solution pipeline for Problem (1); see Figure 5. The Dinkelbach/ Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) analy-sis involved in Theorem 2.9 yields the fixed-point characterisation of the extremal solutions, identifying “ where ” extremal priors assign probability mass in their domain—this deter-mines explicit extremal distributions for obtaining the bounds studied in Lavine (1991) and Moreno and Cano (1991). Consequently, Problem (1) reduces to the finite-dimensional (E1)–(E2) system, which can be solved by Dinkelbach iteration ( cf. (E2) with Example 1 of Wasserman et al. (1993)). The resulting extremal configurations can often be reused to derive other bounds for related problems through symmetry or limiting arguments. Seminal works for KKT analysis are Karush (1939); Kuhn and Tucker (1951), while Nocedal and Wright (2006) contains a modern treatment. In Theorem 2.11, the class C(K) of continuous functions on K is closed under uniform lim-its of continuous functions (including uniformly convergent series of continuous functions), and the objective in (4) depends continuously on f and g under uniform convergence (given inf Ki g > 0 for some i). Hence, analytic approximations give a general solution method for continuous f, g , in (4), even when f, g are defined as uniformly converging series. Proposition 2.12 is useful in extending the results beyond C(K).

Remarks: In the continuous special case ri = 1, Ki, 1 = Ki and Di = ∅ for all i = 1 , . . . , n ,Theorem 2.13 reduces to the fixed-point characterisation of Theorem 2.11. More generally, Theorem 2.13 shows that analytic approximation is “exhaustive”: it identifies the extremal support points, and there are no extremal support candidates outside the class of extremal support points obtained from analytic approximations. Under the assumptions of Proposition 2.12 and Theorem 2.13, Algorithm 2.14 and its convergence proof are analogous (with slight modifications) to Algorithm 2.10.

Fig 5: Methodological pipeline for solving Problem (1) generalisations via discrete extremal-prior reduction, fixed-point support characterisation, and Dinkelbach-type iteration. Each pair ( f − 

> m

, g +

> m

) and ( f +

> m

, g −

> m

) admits simple-function numerator and denominator representations on partitions consisting of finitely many Borel level sets. The common re-finement of the numerator and denominator partitions brings each pair within the scope of Theorem 2.13. For all sufficiently large m, these pairs have benefits: • Systematic construction: “canonical” approximating sequences for f and g.• Certified and convergent bounds: each approximation stage gives a lower and upper bound on the extremal value, and these bounds converge to the extremal value. • Stagewise support characterisation: at stage m, each bounding fixed-point prob-lem has an extremal distribution whose support points provide approximate extremal support locations for the target problem. • Spatial localisation under domain refinement: when the approximations are based on partitions with vanishing mesh (such as our example construction using Π m

with uniformly continuous f , g), every stage-m support point lies in a partition cell whose diameter tends to zero. Along any convergent subsequence of the stagewise support points, the corresponding cells localise the limiting extremal support point. • Generality of value quantisation: when approximations are based on value quan-tisation (e.g. our first example construction using δm) this guarantees uniform approx-imation for every f, g ∈ B+ 

> b

(K). The level-set cells need not have vanishing spatial 19 diameter and, therefore, need not provide comparable spatial localisation compared with “vanishing mesh” approximations. In summary, Theorem 2.15 is a computational route for solving (4) for a general class of functions: solve increasingly accurate approximating problems, use their extremal objec-tive forms guaranteed by Theorem 2.13, recover arbitrarily accurate approximations to ϕ∗,construct asymptotically optimal feasible priors, and identify extremal weak-limit priors for the Theorem 2.15 objective. In particular, i) to approximate ϕ∗, solve the approximating problems from Theorem 2.13 and monitor the convergence of the extremal values ϕ∗ 

> m

→ ϕ∗;

ii) to approximate an extremal prior, one should examine the corresponding extremal sup-port tuples for clustering, convergent subsequences, or approximate stabilisation.

## 3.4. Generalised k-out-of-n Boole–Fréchet Bounds 

Problem (1) and its extensions (via the propositions) significantly generalise Boole-Fréchet bounds. Indeed, the theorems imply that generalised Boole-Fréchet bounds, and their ex-tremal distributions, admit fixed-point characterisations ( cf. Dynamic Programming fixed-point characterisations of Boole-Fréchet bounds in Salako (2025)). For example, consider the following Hailperin/Rüger upper bound on the probability of at least k-out-of-n events occurring (see Rüger (1978)), here given in fixed-point form: 24 0 . . . yj1−2 yj1−1 yj1 yj1+1 . . . yj2−1 yj2 . . . yn−1 1y∗∗ y∗

min {j ∈ { 1, . . . , k − 1} : Uj ⩽ pn−k+1+ j }, if min exists 

k, otherwise Hence, j∗ determines a family of extremal priors with the following property: if an element in the support of the prior represents the occurrence of exactly k-out-of-n marginal Ai events, then it necessarily contains those r∗ = k−j∗ marginal events with the largest pi probabilities; the remaining j∗ marginal events in the support element are drawn from the remaining n−r∗

marginal events with the smallest pi probabilities. Marginal events An−r∗+1 , ..., An (r∗ > 0)are “dominating” events. Proof. Appendix N, Supplementary Material (Salako and Muhammad (2026)). 

## 3.5. Robust Posterior Bounds for Interval-Identified Econometric Models 

Problem (1) has applications in Economics. For example, Theorem 3.3 treats scalar interval-identified models and provides an analogue of Theorem 1 in Giacomini and Kitagawa (2021). Both results express robust posterior bounds through the same underlying containment principle: the lower posterior probability of an event is determined by those values of the identified parameter for which the corresponding identified set is entirely contained in that event. Theorem 3.3 as a whole is not a direct corollary of Giacomini and Kitagawa’s result. Although its robust-bound formula is a specialization of their containment result, its addi-tional contribution is to recast the containment principle in fixed-point and extremal-prior form, yielding a sequence of finite-dimensional extremal problems with explicit finite-support prior representations and convergent bounds for the robust posterior value. The theorem is stated for the infimum; the corresponding supremum result is analogous. 25 φ

Remark: Although Giacomini and Kitagawa establish the existence of feasible priors that attain the robust bound—this can also be established by a modest modification of the fixed-point argument used in proving Theorem 3.3—the formulation developed here i) broadens 26 the solution description to include sequences of feasible priors whose objective values ap-proach the bound, and ii) provides a constructive approximation route for solving the prob-lem. At each approximation level, a finitely supported prior solves, or arbitrarily closely solves, a finite relaxation of the original problem. As partitions of the prior domain are re-fined, the corresponding extremal objective values converge to the robust bound. A subtlety is that a weak limit of these approximating priors may itself fail to attain the robust bound. This is because the objective contains the indicator of the Borel event C, so the objective— viewed as a functional over priors—is not necessarily continuous under weak convergence. Thus, the framework provides a fixed-point characterisation that reveals the structural basis of the robust bound; it also describes finite approximations to the bound and the limiting process by which they approach it. 

## 3.6. Robust Value-at-Risk Aggregation under Prescribed Marginals 

Embrechts et al. (2013) study robust Value-at-Risk (VaR) aggregation under prescribed one-dimensional and higher-dimensional marginal information. Theorem 3.4 characterises extremal probabilities of Borel events over probability measures with uniform coordinate marginals. Corollary 3.5 applies these results to the reduced robust VaR problems de-fined in Embrechts et al. (2013, eqs. (30a)–(30b)), thereby extending their treatment with threshold-event representations, “exchange” fixed-point characterisations of extremal priors, and convergent finite approximations.

Remark: The support characterisation in Theorem 3.4 differs from the other example sup-port characterisations in the framework. In those results, the Dinkelbach difference identi-fies extremal support locations through local or cellwise minimisation conditions. Here, the Dinkelbach difference instead acts on marginal-preserving mass exchanges. Because such exchanges preserve total mass, their Dinkelbach difference reduces to β(G) − α(G), the re-sulting change in event probability. Its vanishing defines the tight exchanges that generate an equivalence class of extremal priors—an “exchange-orbit” of priors with potentially different support—that all attain the extremal event value.

## 3.7. Sharp Lower Bound for Two-Asset Basket Options in Finance 

Consider the two-asset basket option problem studied by Laurence and Wang (2005). In that setting, the Problem (1) objective is the expected payoff of a contract whose value depends on the sum of two underlying quantities. Given only the one-dimensional marginal distributions of those quantities, one seeks the sharpest possible lower and upper bounds on that expected payoff that are consistent with absence of arbitrage. In the two-asset lower-bound case, Laurence and Wang (2005) obtain a closed form. We re-derive this lower bound within the fixed-point framework and highlight the added insights this brings. Let 

K := [0 , 1] 2, D := {P ∈ P (K) : ( π1)#P = λ, (π2)#P = λ} ,

where λ denotes Lebesgue measure on [0 , 1], the π1, π 2 are coordinate projections, and (πi)#P is the push-forward measure of P under πi. Thus, D is the class of all couplings of two Unif[0 , 1] marginals; i.e. the M = 2 special case of Proposition 2.6 with ρ1 ≡ ρ2 ≡ 1and U1 = U2 = [0 , 1]. Remarks: Theorem 3.6 does more than recover the Laurence–Wang lower bound in closed form. It also exhibits a selection mechanism for the extremal branch. In particular, the anti-diagonal/countermonotone solution is not only identified as an optimiser in the limit; it is selected at each finite stage m by recursive uncrossing on dyadic blocks, which yields a canonical anti-diagonal-support representative of the stage-m extremal class. The theorem’s proof also makes the support geometry of the extremals explicit. The selected stage-m extremal representatives are supported on cells contained in the shrinking tube Sm,m ⊆ { (u, v ) ∈ K : |u + v − 1| ⩽ 2−m}, so the anti-diagonal manifold appears as an asymptotic support “attractor” for the extremal equivalence classes selected by the recursion. The Wasserstein estimate W1(Pad  

> m

, Pad 

> m,⋆

) → 0 further shows that the canonical midpoint branch is not merely a convenient visual representative, but is asymptotically equivalent to an exact stage-m extremal solution branch. More broadly, the proof of Theorem 3.6 illustrates a reusable methodological pattern within the fixed-point framework: one first controls theorem-covered stage-m values by lower and upper envelope problems, then uses structural properties of approximants to select canonical surrogate branches (ideally with closed-form limits) that are shown, via a sandwiching argument, to be asymptotically extremal. Thus, beyond the closed-form lower bound itself, the framework supplies a finite-level branch-selection principle, explicit support localisation, and a general approximation strategy for extracting canonical extremisers from broader admissible classes. 

## 3.8. Robust Next-State Inference for a 2-State Markov Chain with Ran-dom Transition Probabilities 

Fix n ⩾ 1, 0 < t < ϵ < 1, 0 < θ < 1, and let Θ = [0 , 1] 1+2 n. Here, n is the number of events in the observed history of a 2-state Markov chain (with states 0 and 1) and Θ is the parameter space for the chain. Write a point q ∈ Θ as q = (q(1) 1 , q (2) 01 , q (2) 11 , . . . , q (n+1) 01 , q (n+1) 11 

). The initial probability of state-1 is q(1) 1 , so that the initial distribution in the chain is 

Q(1) 0 (q) := 1 − q(1) 1 , Q(1) 1 (q) := q(1) 1 .

For i = 2 , . . . , n + 1, define the transition probabilities 

Q(i)01 (q) := q(i)01 , Q(i)00 (q) := 1 − q(i)01 , Q(i)11 (q) := q(i)11 , Q(i)10 (q) := 1 − q(i)11 .

Equivalently, the i-th transition matrix is 

Q(i)(q) := 

(

1 − q(i)01 q(i)01 

1 − q(i)11 q(i)11 

)

, i = 2 , . . . , n + 1 .

Let U = {0, 1}n, so U contains m = 2 n elements. For σ = ( σ(1) , . . . , σ (n)) ∈ U , define the likelihood of observing the n-history σ by 

Gσ (q) = Q(1)  

> σ(1)

(q)

> n

∏

> i=2

# 4. Discussion: Interpretation, Convergence, and Extensions 

For non-trivial problems, the extremal distributions are often “degenerate”: although the parameters may, in principle, take any value in the hypercube K, most parameter values are impossible under these distributions. In Bayesian applications, this degeneracy does not conflict with Bayesian principles. In particular, for conservative Bayesian inference, it is the class of feasible priors defined by the partial prior specifications—rather than any single degenerate extremal prior—that represents one’s prior beliefs. As new observations arrive, every feasible prior is updated, and the extremal priors may change. In the following sense, Bayesian applications of Problem (1) “converge” to traditional Bayesian inference, in the limit of more detailed partial specifications defined over refine-ments of the partition of K (cf. convergence of ϵ-contaminations to traditional Bayesian inference as ϵ → 0; e.g. in Berger (1990), Moreno and Cano (1991)): 

Proposition 4.1 (Convergence to ordinary Bayesian inference) . Let μ ∈ P (K) be a fixed Borel probability measure over K. For each m ∈ N, let K(m) = {K(m)1 , . . . , K (m)

Proof. Appendix S, Supplementary Material (Salako and Muhammad (2026)). 37 This convergence is consistent with the general principle in robust Bayesian inference that the more constrained an inference problem becomes, the tighter the bounds that sandwich the objective function in question (e.g. see Section 4 of Wasserman et al. (1993)). The infimum of the objective function in (4) is attained by certain functional forms related to the objective function: forms ultimately determined by (E1)–(E2) conditions in the Theorems (e.g. Theorem 2.9). An extremal prior assigns probability mass to n locations in its domain—probability pi is assigned to location i. When locations coincide, the probability the prior assigns at the common location is the sum of the corresponding pi’s. Different extremal priors can have different support locations. The i-th location of an extremal prior lies in the closure of the measurable subset Ki, and hence is the limit of points in Ki. Many previously published explicit solutions of (1) have each been constrained by finitely many such functional forms. (E1)–(E2) also make clear how the extremal priors depend on f , g,the pi probabilities, and the partition of K.Disparate analogues of (E1) and (E2) exist in the literature, but without the compu-tational and approximating extremal prior convergence guarantees presented here ( cf. Re-mark 1 of Liseo et al. (1996) for (E1) and Example 1 of Wasserman et al. (1993) for (E2)). The guarantees come at the price of less generality in the classes of f , g functions. A common theme across many of these solutions is that fixed points often behave like “attractors” or “repellers”—attracting or repelling locations where probability masses are assigned in the domain of the prior. The optimisation constraints induce a partition of the prior’s domain, and the fixed points dictate those locations where probability mass should be assigned (in each subset of the partition) to achieve conservative results. For some problems, the fixed point exclusively attracts or repels. For other problems, the fixed point attracts locations in some subsets while repelling locations in other subsets. An example is the five solutions in the proof of the theorem in Zhao et al. (2019); the placement of probability masses for each of these solutions is determined by the attraction and repulsion of the fixed point r/ (r + k) (i.e. the MLE for xr (1 − x)k).  

> 1
> −11
> ϕ∗ϕ∗
> ϕ∗
> rr+krr+krr+krr+m+krr+m+krr+m+k
> y∗∗ y∗∗ y∗∗ y∗y∗y∗
> xh(x)

Fig 17: How ϕ∗, y∗∗ , and y∗ are related by h(x) = (1 − x)m

( r−x(m+k+r)

. Reproduced from Salako and Muhammad (2025). As a particular example, the components of Theorem 3.1’s fixed–point triplet — i.e. ϕ∗,

y∗∗ , y∗ — are related by the function h : [0 , 1] \ { rr+k } → R, h(x) = (1 − x)m

( r−x(m+k+r)

;see Figure 17. The gradient of the objective function defines h, where h determines when 38 gradient components are non-trivially zero. The roots of the difference h − ϕ, between h and the objective function ϕ, imply the fixed point “attracts” (w.r.t. y∗) and “repels” (w.r.t. y∗∗ ). In terms of the more general setting of Theorem 2.9, the h − ϕ difference is a rescaling of the Dinkelbach slope ∇hϕ,f,g (x) (see Definition 2.1), with the sign of the scaling factor changing at x = r/ (r + k). In this one-dimensional example, the “repeller” y∗∗ < r/ (r + k) is a root at which ∇hϕ∗,f,g (x) passes monotonically from positive to negative, whereas the “attractor” 

y∗ > r/ (r + k) is a root at which it passes monotonically from negative to positive. More generally, any interior point x∗ 

> i

satisfying (E1) of Theorem 2.9 is a local “attractor” in this terminology, since it is a local minimiser of hϕ∗,f,g . See Appendix G of the Supplementary Material (Salako and Muhammad (2026)). As highlighted by Theorem 2.9, “attractors” and “repellers” are not necessarily “points”; they can be “line segments”, “surfaces”, or topological manifolds in the prior’s domain. For example, the extremal priors for (14) have line segments in their domain that “attract” univariate probability density (e.g. see Salako and Zhao (2023)). Another example arises in Problem (48) and its solution in Theorem 3.6 (extending the solution in Laurence and Wang (2005)): the attractor is the anti-diagonal of the unit square (see Figure 15), which is the support of the limiting extremal distribution. More-over, for non-negative X and Y , the classical lower bound for the E[XY ] problem with prescribed marginals (see Whitt (1976)) can be recovered; e.g. consider the following special case obtained in a manner directly analogous to Theorem 3.6. Writing X = F −1(U ) and 

Y = G−1(V ), where F −1 and G−1 denote quantile functions that extend to continuous func-tions on [0 , 1] and U, V ∼ Unif(0 , 1), the problem reduces to minimizing E[F −1(U )G−1(V )]

over all couplings of U and V . The finite marginal approximants lead to the same un-crossing structure as in Theorem 3.6: mass assigned to positively ordered pairs can be re-arranged to oppositely ordered pairs without increasing the objective. Consequently, the extremal approximants concentrate on the anti-diagonal in quantile coordinates; in the limit the extremal prior is supported on {(u, v ) ∈ [0 , 1] 2 : v = 1 − u}. The lower bound is inf X∼F, Y ∼G E[XY ] = ∫ 10 F −1(u)G−1(1 − u) du . Equivalently, in ( X, Y )-coordinates, the anti-diagonal is mapped to the one-dimensional curve u 7 → (F −1(u), G −1(1 − u)): the in-termediate extremal ( X, Y )-priors concentrate on this curve, generalising the Theorem 3.6 anti-diagonal attractor. Theorems 3.3 and 3.4 offer more general examples of “attractors”—Borel subsets of the unit square and unit hypercube, respectively. The K ∩ Cc attractor in Theorem 3.3 only attracts probability mass that can reach it through vertical movement, leaving the remaining mass in vertical sections from which no such escape is possible; see Figure 13. In Theorem 3.4, mass is attracted from multiple locations and assigned to multiple locations (some lying within the attractor G) only when all of these locations form part of an admissible mass exchange: see Figure 14. The exchange formulation of Theorem 3.4 also reveals a two-level orbit structure. At the level of admissibility, every admissible prior in the fixed-marginal class D can be obtained from any other admissible prior by a complete marginal-preserving mass exchange; thus, viewed from any P ∈ D , the entire feasible class D is P’s complete exchange orbit. At the level of extremality, whenever the target event-probability bound is attained, the exchanges having zero Dinkelbach difference—the tight exchanges—generate the complete family of ex-tremal priors from any extremal prior P∗. The extremal solution set is, in general, a smaller tight-exchange orbit nested within the admissible exchange orbit. This global classification sharpens the attractor interpretation above. An extremising attractor includes the target event G and describes the objective-improving direction of admissible mass exchange: mass 39 can be reassigned so as to increase P(G) only through exchanges that preserve the prescribed marginals. The orbit structure describes the corresponding reachability: the complete ex-change orbit through any admissible prior is the entire feasible class, and the tight-exchange orbit through any extremal prior is the entire extremal prior class; see Theorem 3.4(a)–(b), Figure 14, and Appendix P, Steps 1 and 6. Corollary 3.5 carries the same structure into prescribed-marginal robust VaR problems. Degenerate extremal distributions supported on lower-dimensional manifolds also occur for multidimensional ϵ-contamination problems; e.g. 2-dimensional extreme priors with sup-port on 1-dimensional curves in the priors’ domains—see Theorem 1 and Remark 1 of Liseo et al. (1996), Remark 2 of Moreno and Cano (1995), and Lavine et al. (1991). Most of the results of this paper are subject to finite or discretised setwise mass con-straints: Borel partition masses, overlapping Borel-event masses, fixed marginal laws ex-pressed as cylinder-set probabilities, and combinations/refinements/limits of these. A no-table exception is Proposition 2.2, which considers moment constraints. A natural extension would be more general linear integral constraints (e.g. see Karr (1983); Betrò et al. (1994)) where one fixes finitely many quantities of the form E[fi] = ci; then, the current paper’s finite atom-mass polytopes arising from Borel refinements would be replaced, or augmented, by consistency polytopes that encode the values of constraint functions on refined atoms. Karr-type results describe which finite supports can be extremal, while extensions using this fixed-point framework could potentially also identify where extremal support should con-centrate, and approximate the corresponding extremal distributions by recursively solving related finite discretised problems.

Proof. Fix P ∈ D p, m and set ϕP := EP[f (X)]  

> EP[g(X)]

. Since f, g ⩾ 0 and EP[g(X)] > 0, we have 

ϕP ⩾ 0. By Definition 2.1, the Dinkelbach difference is hϕP,f,g (x) = f (x) − ϕPg(x). Note that 

EP[hϕP,f,g (X)] = EP[f (X)] − ϕPEP[g(X)] = 0 .

Define the constraint map T : K → Rn+q by 

T (x) := (1{x∈K1}, . . . , 1{x∈Kn}, ψ 1(x), . . . , ψ q (x)) .

The first n coordinates satisfy ∑ni=1 1{x∈Ki} = 1 for all x ∈ K, so the set T (K) lies in an affine subspace of dimension at most n − 1 + q.Now, extend T by adjoining the Dinkelbach difference. Define YϕP : K → Rn+q+1 by 

YϕP (x) := ( T (x), h ϕP,f,g (x)). Adjoining hϕP,f,g increases affine dimension by at most one, so YϕP (K) lies in an affine subspace of dimension at most n + q. Moreover, EP[YϕP (X)] = (p1, . . . , p n, m 1, . . . , m q , 0). By Lemma A.1, applied to YϕP and the probability measure P,there exist points x1, . . . , xN ∈ K, with N ⩽ n + q + 1, and weights w1, . . . , w N ⩾ 0, ∑Nℓ=1 wℓ = 1, such that ∑Nℓ=1 wℓYϕP (xℓ) = EP[YϕP (X)]. Lemma A.1 (Convex form of Richter’s Theorem (Richter (1957), Satz 4)) . Let (S, A, μ ) be a probability space and let Y : S → Rr be an integrable measurable map. Suppose that Y (S)

is contained in an affine subspace of Rr of dimension at most d. Then, there exist points 

s1, . . . , s N ∈ S and weights w1, . . . , w N ⩾ 0, with N ⩽ d + 1 and ∑Nℓ=1 wℓ = 1 , such that 

∫ 

> S

Y (s) dμ (s) = ∑Nℓ=1 wℓY (sℓ).Proof. Let y := ∫ 

> S

Y (s) dμ (s), A := Y (S), C := conv( A), C := cl( C). Here, conv( A) denotes the set of all finite convex combinations of points of A.We first show that y ∈ C. Suppose, to the contrary, that y /∈ C. Since C is a closed convex subset of the finite-dimensional Euclidean space Rr , there exist u ∈ Rr and α ∈ R

46 such that u · y < α ⩽ u · z for every z ∈ C. Since Y (s) ∈ A ⊆ C for every s ∈ S, this gives 

u · Y (s) ⩾ α for every s ∈ S. Integrating yields 

u · y = u ·

∫

> S

Y (s) dμ (s) = 

∫

> S

u · Y (s) dμ (s) ⩾ α, 

which contradicts u · y < α . Hence, y ∈ C.We next show that, in fact, y ∈ C. This is proved by induction on d0 := dim L, where 

L is the smallest affine space containing C; i.e. L := aff( C). By the definitions of A, C,and the fact that affine subspaces of Rr are closed, we have aff( C) = aff( A). Thus, if Y (S)is contained in an affine subspace of dimension at most d, then d0 ⩽ d. The induction hypothesis is that, for any integrable measurable map whose associated closed convex hull has affine dimension strictly smaller than d0, the integral of that map belongs to the convex hull of its image. If d0 = 0, then C consists of a single point. Since C is nonempty and C ⊆ C, it follows that C = C, and therefore y ∈ C. Assume now that d0 ⩾ 1 and that the assertion has already been proved in all smaller affine dimensions. Since y ∈ C, either y ∈ ri( C) or 

y ∈ rbd( C), where both relative interior ri( C) and relative boundary rbd( C) are taken in the affine space L.Suppose first that y ∈ ri( C). By the definition of relative interior, there exists ρ > 0 such that the relative open ball BL(y, ρ ) := {z ∈ L : ∥z − y∥ < ρ } is contained in C. Construct 

z0, . . . , zd0 that lie in BL(y, ρ ) ⊆ C as follows. Let V := L − y be the d0-dimensional linear space parallel to L, and choose a basis v1, . . . , vd0 of V . Define

Therefore, y ∈ C.It remains to consider the case y ∈ rbd( C). By the supporting-hyperplane theorem (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004, Section 2.5.2) in the affine space L, there exists u ∈ Rr whose restriction to the direction space of L is not identically zero, such that u · z ⩾ u · y for every 

z ∈ C. Consequently, u · Y (s) ⩾ u · y for every s ∈ S. Define Z(s) := u · Y (s) − u · y. Then,

Since the restriction of u to the direction space of L is not identically zero, H is a proper affine hyperplane of L. Hence, dim aff( Y (S0)) < d 0. The extremal prior can assign probability masses to, at most, n + 1 atoms in its domain. Hence, the form of the extremal prior must be as stated. Proof. The following three-step proof is reproduced from Salako and Muhammad (2025): 

step I: the gradient of the objective function determines the functional forms the objective function can assume when minimized. Prove that two of the gradient’s com-ponents each have a non-trivial root in [0 , 1] when these components equal zero, then use these roots and the signs of the gradient’s components to deduce finitely many functional forms for the objective function when minimized. 

step II: prove these functional forms have finitely many infima, thus the objective function has finitely many infima: the smallest infimum is the global infimum. 

step III: based on the previous steps, state the infimum, state (15)’s solution as a fixed–point system, and state the prior that gives the infimum. 

Step I: There are a finite number of plausible functional forms that ϕ can take when minimized. To deduce these, first, we prove the existence of a pair of points at which two components of the gradient of ϕ are zero. Then, using this pair to deduce the signs of gradient components, we deduce the functional forms. Define f, g : [0 , 1] −→ [0 , 1], f (x) = (1 −x)mg(x) and g(x) = xr (1 −x)k, with k > 0, r > 0. Consider xi ∈ Ki for i = 1 , . . . , n . The gradient of ϕ has i-th component 

∂ϕ ∂x i

= g(xi)( r − xi(r + k)) pi

xi(1 − xi)∑nj=1 g(xj )pj

(h(xi) − ϕ) , (87) where h is the function h : [0 , 1] \ { rr+k } −→ R, h(x) = (1 − x)m

( r−x(m+k+r)

> r−x(k+r)

)

. From (87), the i-th gradient component is non-trivially zero if and only if 

h(xi) = ϕ (88) Otherwise, the gradient component is positive or negative, depending on combinations of whether h(xi) > ϕ or h(xi) < ϕ , and xi < rr+k or xi > rr+k .The signs of the gradient components determine a preferred location in each compact interval Ki (Ki is the closure of Ki) where probability mass should be assigned to mini-mize ϕ. This restricts the possible functional forms of ϕ that equal the infimum, ϕ∗, of ϕ.Probability pi could be placed at yi−1 if ∂ϕ/∂x i > 0 in a neighborhood of yi−1, or it could be placed at yi if ∂ϕ/∂x i < 0 in a neighborhood of yi. It could also be placed at x, if x is a stationary point (i.e. ∂ϕ/∂x i = 0 at xi = x) at which ϕ has a local infimum. Whenever the pis are assigned within each Ki, there are only two stationary points in [0 , 1] that satisfy ∂ϕ/∂x i = 0 — one point which is a local infimum, and the other a local supremum. This follows from the graph of h, which we deduce now. 

h is continuously differentiable on its domain. The derivative of h shows h is monoton-ically decreasing over [0 , rr+m+k ) and ( rr+k , 1], and any stationary points of h must lie in ( rr+m+k , rr+k ). Differentiating h for x̸ = rr+k ,

dh

( r−x(r+m+k)

< 0. Thus, 

dh/ dx < 0 for x < rr+m+k , because r − x(r + m + k) > 0 and r − x(r + k) > 0 whenever x < 

77 rr+m+k . Similarly, dh/ dx < 0 for x > rr+k , because r − x(r + m + k) < 0 and r − x(r + k) <

0 whenever x > rr+k . So, h is monotonically decreasing over these intervals, as claimed. However, for rr+m+k < x < rr+k (i.e. r − x(r + m + k) < 0 and r − x(r + k) > 0), the sign of dh/ dx depends on r, k , and m.Interestingly, h(x) < 0 over the interval rr+m+k < x < rr+k . Furthermore, h has, at most, a pair of stationary points in this interval; because, from (89), dh/ dx = 0 non-trivially if and only if 

x = 2r2 + (2 k + m + 1) r ± √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2

2( r + k)( r + m + k)These stationary points are real iff (−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2) ⩾ 0. Otherwise, there are no stationary points: the points are a complex conjugate pair. When real, this pair of points lies in ( rr+m+k , rr+k ). The smaller of the two points is greater than rr+m+k , because 2r2 + (2 k + m + 1) r − √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2

2( r + k)( r + m + k) > rr + m + k

⇐⇒ r(m + 1) − √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2 > 0The last inequality is true because r(m + 1) > r (m − 1). Analogously, the larger of the two points is less than rr+k , because 2r2 + (2 k + m + 1) r + √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2

2( r + k)( r + m + k) < rr + k

⇐⇒ −r(m − 1) + √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2

2( r + k)( r + m + k) < 0

⇐⇒ − r(m − 1) + √−4rk 2 − 4kr (m + r) + r2(m − 1) 2 < 0The last inequality is true because the radicand subtracts the positive number 4 rk 2+4 kr (m+

r) from r2(m − 1) 2, so the square-root of the radicand must be smaller than r(m − 1). We conclude that both stationary points of h lie in ( rr+m+k , rr+k ). So far, we have shown h is monotonically decreasing on [0 , rr+m+k ) and ( rr+k , 1]. Notice, from the definition of h, h(x) ⩾ 0 over these intervals. We have also shown h has a pair of stationary points (if any) in ( rr+m+k , rr+k ). And, over this interval, h < 0 by definition. Altogether, this means (88) has a root — ϕ can equal h — only where h is positive and monotonically decreasing; that is, only over [0 , 1] \ ( rr+m+k , rr+k ). In fact, there are two reasons why 0 < ϕ < 1, so that ϕ is bounded by h: i) the terms in ϕ’s numerator are strictly less than the corresponding terms in its denominator; ii) both numerator and denominator are strictly positive. Figure 18 illustrates the graph of h. There is an x∗ ∈ ( rr+k , 1] such that h(x∗) = 1. So, h is 0 at 1 and rr+m+k , while h is 1 at 0 and x∗. Moreover, for an arbitrary value of ϕ, denoted ˆϕ,this value is represented as a horizontal line at height ˆϕ. And, for any xu ∈ [x∗, 1] such that 

h(xu) = ˆϕ, a corresponding xl ∈ [0 , rr+m+k ] also satisfies h(xl) = ˆϕ. Since h bounds ϕ, and the horizontal line of height ˆϕ intersects h at exactly two points, these imply the functional form of ϕ when minimized must be based on exactly two components of the gradient of 

ϕ vanishing. The local supremum acts like a “source” that “repels” probability masses in 78 nearby intervals, while the local infimum acts like a “sink” that “attracts” probability masses. This proves that at most two components of the gradient have non-trivial roots.

. Reproduced from Salako and Muhammad (2025). Consequently, to minimise ϕ, all of the xi must be assigned in their respective Ki as dictated by a “source” and “sink” pair. All xi to the left of the “source” must be located at the lower endpoints of their respective Ki intervals, while xi between the “source” and “sink” must be located at the upper endpoints of their Ki. The xi for the interval containing the “source” must be located at one of the endpoints of the interval — whichever endpoint gives the lower objective function value. Analogous reasoning in terms of the “sink” requires all xi to the right of the “sink” to be located at the lower endpoints of their respective Ki.The xi in the same interval as the “sink” should be located at the “sink”. By Proposition 2.12, the atomised domain may be further refined without changing the objective’s optimal value, and by Theorem 2.13 the corresponding refined problems admit the (E1)–(E2) extremal solution characterisation. For each j = 1 , . . . , k , define the stage-j indicator objective by

where mj = n − k + j. Thus, fj records failure of a j-out-of-mj objective using only the smallest mj marginals. This finite sequence of approximants converges pointwise to f in finitely many steps, so the natural extension to an infinite sequence converges uniformly to 

f .The proof now uses the following three observations in four proof steps. 

Observation 1. After atomisation via Proposition 2.3, the outermost optimisation can be chosen to enforce ∑ 

> I

pI = 1, with the inner optimisation producing uncapped solutions that must satisfy the marginal event constraints; the outer problem then truncates the inner solution at 1. 

Observation 2. For the inner optimisation, one may restrict attention to exactly k-contributor atoms: if a successful atom has m > k contributors, then m − k contributors may have their 83 mass reassigned to atoms consisting of only one marginal event, while the remaining k con-tributors may have their mass reassigned to a k-atom consisting only of these k contributors, without decreasing the objective value. 

Observation 3. For each j = 1 , . . . , k , one may consider the stage-j reduced objective ob-tained by counting only the smallest mj = n − k + j marginals as genuinely contributing to the objective function. Equivalently, the largest k − j marginals are treated as already dominating. 

Step 1: stage j contributes exactly one new quotient form. Fix j ∈ { 1, . . . , k }

and consider the stage-j problem determined by fj . By Observation 2, we may analyse only exact-k atoms. Inside stage j, there may be dominating marginals among the smallest mj

eligible marginals. Suppose there are s ∈ { 0, . . . , j − 1} such dominating marginals. Then the number of non-dominating contributors is 

t := j − s ∈ { 1, . . . , j }.

Accordingly, the total number of dominating marginals if these j-atoms are extended trivially to k-atoms is ( k − j) + s = k − t. Hence, the set of non-dominating marginals has cardinality 

n−(k −t) = n−k +t. Summing the marginal constraints over the non-dominating marginals counts each successful exact-k atom exactly t times. Therefore, the corresponding (E2) quotient must have the uncapped form 

∑n−k+ti=1 pi

t

and, by Observation 1, it is capped at 1. Thus, the stage-j candidate optimal objective values are precisely 

Ut = min 

{ ∑n−k+ti=1 pi

t , 1

}

, t = 1 , . . . , j. 

That is, stage j contributes one new form Uj , with its total candidate set being {U1, . . . , U j }.For j = k, one has mk = n, so fk = f and Observation 3 imposes no further restriction: the stage-k problem is exactly the original inner exact-k problem obtained after Observation 2. 

Step 2: each Uj is an upper bound. Fix j ∈ { 1, . . . , k }. Again by Observation 2, it is enough to count only exact-k successful atoms. Any exact-k atom contains at most 

k − j indices outside the smallest mj = n − k + j marginals. Hence, every exact-k atom must contain at least j indices from {1, . . . , m j }. So, summing the marginal constraints over {1, . . . , m j } counts each exact-k atom at least j times, so j Φk ⩽ ∑mj

Since R is Borel, it is analytic and therefore capacitable: Cap( R) = sup {Cap( F ) : F ⊆ R, F compact } .

Proof. We organise the proof around the two constrained optimisation problems in (21). Since I(G) = 1 − V (Gc), it is sufficient to solve the maximizing problem 

ϕ+ 

> G

= sup 

> P∈D

P(G). (101) The minimizing problem then follows by applying the same arguments to Gc.The proofs of Theorem 3.4 (including (101)) and Corollary 3.5 proceed through four successive stages. 1. Reduction of the constrained feasible set to exchange space. Starting from a candidate prior P ∈ D , we show that every feasible competitor in D is obtained from P by a com-plete admissible mass exchange. The constrained optimisation problem can therefore be rewritten as an optimisation over X(P). This reduction produces the exchange-space Dinkelbach difference, the global ( E1) G–( E2) G system, and the residual identity in part (a). 2. Reduction to marginal-completion fibres. To determine what global extremality implies for the support and masses of an extremal prior, we apply the same exchange reduction to every removable submeasure 0 < α ⩽ P∗. The resulting constrained problem is the optimisation of β(G) over C(α). Its Dinkelbach form yields the fibrewise fixed-point conditions, hereditary fibre-extremality, and the tight-exchange description of the complete extremal family in part (b). 3. Finite approximation of Borel-event problems. Since G is Borel (in particular, ana-lytic), we first approximate G from within by increasing compact sets Fℓ. For each fixed compact core, we then construct a nested family of dyadic whole-cell optimisa-tion problems. Their feasible points are represented by cell-mass tables, and each such table admits a realisation in D with the same whole-cell objective value. This yields the discretised extremal values appearing in part (c). 4. Passage to limits and application to robust VaR aggregation. For each compact core, weak compactness, together with the dyadic bounded-Lipschitz estimate, yields a se-quence of discrete extremal priors converging weakly to a prior attaining the compact-core extremal value. The compact-core and discretisation limits then prove part (c). For the VaR application, the paired quantile versions in (28) generate the two threshold-event families Cs and Gs in (32). The compact-target arguments above apply directly to both families, yielding attained extremal probabilities, exchange fixed-point pairs, and convergent finite fixed-marginal approximations. The symmetric representations in (30) then identify the lower and upper robust VaR bounds with the corresponding threshold inversions. Compact-set continuity closes the upper feasible-threshold set and shows that its extremal threshold, and hence the robust upper VaR, is attained. This proves Corollary 3.5. We now carry out these four stages. 

I. Reduction of the constrained feasible set to exchange space. 

Step 1: Complete exchanges describe exactly the feasible competitors in (101) .

Let P ∈ D and ( α, β ) ∈ X(P). Since 0 ⩽ α ⩽ P, one has P−α ⩾ 0. Thus, Tα,β P = P−α+β

is a positive measure. Moreover, (Tα,β P)(K) = 1 − α(K) + β(K) = 1. For every j = 1 , . . . , d ,(pr j )#Tα,β P = (pr j )#P − (pr j )#α + (pr j )#β = λ. Hence, 

Tα,β P ∈ D . (102) 93 The corresponding change in the target objective is 

(Tα,β P)(G) − P(G) = β(G) − α(G) = ∆ G(α, β ). (103) Conversely, let Q ∈ D be an arbitrary feasible prior in (101) different from P. Take the Jordan decomposition Q − P = β − α, where α and β are mutually-singular non-negative measures. Since α = −(Q − P)|N where N is a negative set of the decomposition, we have 

α(A) = P(A ∩ N ) − Q(A ∩ N ) ⩽ P(A ∩ N ) ⩽ P(A) for all Borel sets A. Hence, 0 ⩽ α ⩽ P.An analogous argument shows 0 ⩽ β ⩽ Q. Since Q and P also have the same coordinate marginals, (pr j )#(Q − P) = 0 implies (pr j )#β = (pr j )#α by the linearity of push-forwards, for j = 1 , . . . , d . We conclude that ( α, β ) ∈ X(P) and Q = Tα,β P.Therefore the feasible set of (101), viewed from any P ∈ D , is precisely its complete exchange orbit: 

D = {Tα,β P : ( α, β ) ∈ X(P)} . (104) Consequently, 

V (G) = P(G) + sup 

> (α,β )∈X(P)

∆G(α, β ). (105) This is the fundamental reduction of the target constrained problem to the fixed-point framework. 

Step 2: The global (E1) G–(E2) G system solves the target problem. 

Suppose first that P∗ solves (101), and put ϕ∗ := P∗(G). For every ( α, β ) ∈ X(P∗), (102) gives Tα,β P∗ ∈ D . Optimality therefore implies (Tα,β P∗)(G) ⩽ P∗(G) = ϕ∗. Equivalently, 

hP∗

> ϕ∗,G

(α, β ) ⩽ 0. The zero exchange has value hP∗

> ϕ∗,G

(0 , 0) = P∗(G) − ϕ∗ = 0. Hence ( E1) G

holds, while ( E2) G holds by the definition of ϕ∗.Conversely, suppose that ( P∗, ϕ ∗) satisfies ( E1) G and ( E2) G. Let Q ∈ D . By (104), there exists ( α, β ) ∈ X(P∗) such that Q = Tα,β P∗. Using ( E2) G, condition ( E1) G yields 0 ⩾ hP∗

> ϕ∗,G

(α, β ) = Q(G) − P∗(G). Thus, Q(G) ⩽ P∗(G) for all Q ∈ D . Therefore, P∗ solves (101) and ϕ∗ = P∗(G) = V (G). This proves the fixed-point characterisation in part (a). 

Step 3: The exchange Dinkelbach difference and the global residual. 

By (103), the Dinkelbach difference associated with a feasible exchange is ∆ G(α, β ) = 

β(G) − α(G). For every ( α, β ) ∈ X(P), Tα,β P ∈ D , and hence ∆ G(α, β ) = (Tα,β P)(G) −

P(G) ⩽ V (G) − P(G). Taking the supremum gives RG(P) ⩽ V (G) − P(G). Conversely, for every Q ∈ D , Step 1 produces an admissible exchange such that ∆ G(α, β ) = 

Q(G) − P(G). Taking the supremum over Q ∈ D gives RG(P) ⩾ V (G) − P(G). Therefore 

RG(P) = V (G) − P(G). It follows immediately that RG(P) = 0 ⇐⇒ P ∈ arg max Q∈D Q(G), and Pr (G) −→ 

V (G) ⇐⇒ RG(Pr ) −→ 0. Thus, the residual RG(P) is not merely a local stationarity quantity: over the complete exchange class it is exactly the remaining value in the target optimisation problem. If the residual is instead computed over a proper exchange subfamily, its vanishing establishes only stability with respect to that subfamily unless the subfamily is proved to generate every feasible displacement in (104). This completes the proof of part (a) for the maximizing problem. The minimizing asser-tions follow by replacing G with Gc and using I(G) = 1 − V (Gc).

Step 5: Hereditary fibre-extremality characterises extremal priors. 

Suppose that P∗ solves (101). Let 0 < α ⩽ P∗ and let β ∈ C (α). Then P∗ − α + β ∈ D .Optimality of P∗ gives P∗(G) − α(G) + β(G) ⩽ P∗(G). Therefore, β(G) ⩽ α(G) for all

Take α = P∗. Since the marginals of P∗ are all λ, C(P∗) = D. Consequently, P∗(G) = Γ G(P∗) = sup Q∈D Q(G) = 

V (G). Thus, P∗ solves the original target problem. This proves the hereditary extremal prior characterisation in part (b). 

Step 6: Tight exchanges describe the complete solution set. 

Let P∗ be an extremiser. By the global fixed-point condition, ∆ G(α, β ) ⩽ 0 for all ( α, β ) ∈

X(P∗). If ∆ G(α, β ) = 0, then (Tα,β P∗)(G) = P∗(G) = V (G), so the exchanged prior is also an extremiser. Conversely, let Q∗ be any other solution of (101). By Step 1, the Jordan decomposition 

Q∗ − P∗ = β − α gives an admissible exchange from P∗. Since both priors are extremal, ∆G(α, β ) = Q∗(G) − P∗(G) = 0. Thus, arg max P∈D P(G) = {P∗ − α + β : ( α, β ) ∈ X0

> G

(P∗)}.Therefore, zero Dinkelbach difference identifies exchanges tangent to the extremal solution set, while negative Dinkelbach difference identifies strictly worsening feasible displacements. This completes the proof of part (b). 

III. Finite approximation of Borel-event problems. The preceding arguments identify the structure of the set of extremisers of (101). Since the Borel event G is analytic, direct attainment need not be available. We therefore approximate G from within by compact-core problems and approximate each compact-core problem by finite whole-cell fixed-point problems.
